<div id="abstract1" class="ltx_abstract">

###### Abstract

Equipping Large Language Model (LLM) agents with domain-specific skills
is critical for tackling complex tasks. Yet, manual authoring creates a
severe scalability bottleneck. Conversely, automated skill generation
often yields fragile or fragmented results because it either relies on
shallow parametric knowledge or sequentially overfits to
non-generalizable trajectory-local lessons. To overcome this, we
introduce Trace2Skill, a framework that mirrors how human experts author
skills: by holistically analyzing broad execution experience before
distilling it into a single, comprehensive guide. Instead of reacting
sequentially to individual trajectories, Trace2Skill dispatches a
parallel fleet of sub-agents to analyze a diverse pool of executions. It
extracts trajectory-specific lessons and hierarchically consolidates
them into a unified, conflict-free skill directory via inductive
reasoning. Trace2Skill supports both deepening existing human-written
skills and creating new ones from scratch. Experiments in challenging
domains, such as spreadsheet, VisionQA and math reasoning, show that
Trace2Skill significantly improves upon strong baselines, including
Anthropic’s official xlsx skills. Crucially, this trajectory-grounded
evolution does not merely memorize task instances or model-specific
quirks: evolved skills transfer across LLM scales and generalize to OOD
settings. For example, skills evolved by Qwen3.5-35B on its own
trajectories improved a Qwen3.5-122B agent by up to 57.65 absolute
percentage points on WikiTableQuestions. Further analysis confirms that
our holistic, parallel consolidation outperforms both online sequential
editing and retrieval-based experience banks. Ultimately, our results
demonstrate that complex agent experience can be packaged into highly
transferable, declarative skills—requiring no parameter updates, no
external retrieval modules, and utilizing open-source models as small as
35B
parameters.<span class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup>1</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>1</sup><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">1</span>Work
in progress.</span></span></span>

</div>

<div id="S1" class="section ltx_section" lang="en">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">1 </span>Introduction

<figure id="S1.F1" class="ltx_figure ltx_align_floatright">
<img src="/html/2603.25158/assets/x1.png" id="S1.F1.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="830"
height="380" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S1.F1.4.1.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 1</span>:
</span><em>Left:</em><span id="S1.F1.6.3" class="ltx_text"
style="font-size:90%;"> Concurrent work’s online setting where lessons
from in-coming traces evolve a skill bank sequentially. <em>Right:</em>
Trace2Skill’s analyze a pool of traces in parallel, and hierarchically
consolidating lessons to induce generalizable SOPs.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S1.p1" class="ltx_para">

LLM-based agents are increasingly rely on *skills* — structured,
reusable documents that encode task-solving procedures, domain
knowledge, and operational guidelines — to navigate complex environments
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">anthropic2026skills</span>).
As these agents are deployed across increasingly broad and nuanced
domain-specific use cases, demand for highly specialized skills grows
accordingly, creating a scalability bottleneck for manual skill creation
and maintenance
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">han2026sweskillsbenchagentskillsactually</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">li2026organizingorchestratingbenchmarkingagent</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">anthropic2026skillcreatorconversation</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">liang2026skillnetcreateevaluateconnect</span>).
Even when a human-written skill exists, it is not guaranteed to improve
performance for a given agent, model, or task distribution (e.g., Table
<a href="#S3.T1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 1 ‣ Implementation Details. ‣ 3.1 Experimental Setup ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a> shows that a
human-expert-written skill that lifts a 122B agent by $`+`$<!-- -->20 pp
on SpreadsheetBench-Verified
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">ma2024spreadsheetbenchchallengingrealworld</span>)
actively harms a 35B agent). These pressures motivate automatic
*creation* and *adaptation* of skills for specific use cases
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">han2026sweskillsbenchagentskillsactually</span>).

</div>

<div id="S1.p2" class="ltx_para">

However, synthesizing skills relying solely on an LLM’s parametric
knowledge yields limited benefits, even with leading proprietary models,
primarily because parametric knowledge lacks information about the
specifics and common pitfalls of the target domain
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">li2026skillsbenchbenchmarkingagentskills</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">jiang2026xskillcontinuallearningexperience</span>).
To address this, concurrent work proposes improving skills using agent
execution experience in an online setting, where an agent continuously
interacts with the environment and evolves its skill collection based on
incoming trajectories
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">yang2026autoskillexperiencedrivenlifelonglearning</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">xia2026skillrlevolvingagentsrecursive</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">alzubi2026evoskillautomatedskilldiscovery</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">zhou2026mementoskillsletagentsdesign</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">jiang2026xskillcontinuallearningexperience</span>).

</div>

<div id="S1.p3" class="ltx_para">

While this continuous, online paradigm has shown promise,
<span id="S1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">we approach the
problem of skill evolution from a different angle—one that more closely
mirrors how human experts author skills.</span> Specifically, we observe
that existing online paradigms often diverge from human methodology in
two key ways:

</div>

<div id="S1.p4" class="ltx_para">

- <span id="S1.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S1.I1.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Skill
  Fragmentation vs. Consolidation:</span> Existing works often create
  new, narrowly tailored skills to host trajectory-local lessons,
  resulting in massive skill collections that can lead to retrieval
  difficulties
  (<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">li2026singleagentskillsreplacemultiagent</span>).
  In contrast, human experts typically craft a single, comprehensive
  skill per domain, complete with broad procedural guidance and error
  prevention checklists.

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S1.I1.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Sequential
  vs. Holistic Updates:</span> In an online setting, skills are updated
  sequentially using lessons from isolated incoming trajectories
  (<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">jiang2026xskillcontinuallearningexperience</span>;
  <span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">xia2026skillrlevolvingagentsrecursive</span>).
  This mimics a scenario where an author continuously edits a skill
  while sequentially learning about a domain, reacting prematurely
  before acquiring adequate domain-specific knowledge. Human experts,
  conversely, build a comprehensive, high-level understanding of the
  domain before instantiating it into a skill. Figure
  <a href="#S1.F1" class="ltx_ref"
  title="Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
  class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a> illustrates these
  comparisons.

  </div>

</div>

<div id="S1.p5" class="ltx_para">

Motivated by these observations, we introduce Trace2Skill, a framework
designed to simulate this human, holistic approach. Rather than reacting
to trajectories sequentially, Trace2Skill analyzes a wide range of
trajectory-local lessons in parallel, and distills common patterns into
a single, comprehensive agent skill. Trace2Skill operates in three
stages: (1) <span id="S1.p5.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Trajectory Generation:</span> An agent
runs in parallel on an evolving set of tasks, producing a pool of
execution trajectories. (2) <span id="S1.p5.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Parallel Multi-Agent Patch
Proposal:</span> A fleet of success and error-analyst sub-agents
independently processes batches of trajectories, proposing targeted
patches to the skill. (3) <span id="S1.p5.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Conflict-Free Consolidation:</span>
Sub-agent-proposed patches are hierarchically merged into a coherent
update to the skill directory, utilizing programmatic conflict detection
and format validation at each step.

</div>

<div id="S1.p6" class="ltx_para">

We process all patches simultaneously during consolidation for two
reasons. First, this acts as an inductive reasoning process
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">xiong-etal-2025-co</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">li2025mirageevaluatingexplaininginductive</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">lin2025llmbasedscientificinductivereasoning</span>)
that mines generalizable patterns from experience-specific patches,
building a high-level understanding of the domain analogous to a human
expert’s prior knowledge. Second, analyzing a massive number of
trajectories in parallel brings substantial efficiency benefits and
ensures a holistic view of the domain. This reflects the core design
wisdom of agent swarms
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">kimi2026agentswarm</span>),
which process multiple information sources efficiently using
parallelized sub-agents. The framework supports two modes: deepening an
existing human-written skill, and creating an effective skill from
scratch starting from an ineffective LLM-generated draft.

</div>

<div id="S1.p7" class="ltx_para">

The most surprising finding of this work is not just that trajectory
analysis improves skill quality, but that it does so without sacrificing
generalizability. Despite the deep analysis over a specific task
distribution and trajectories of a specific LLM, evolved skills transfer
across model scales (e.g., a skill evolved by Qwen3.5-35B
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">qwen35blog</span>)
improves Qwen3.5-122B) and generalize to out-of-distribution task
domains (e.g. from spreadsheet editing to Wikipedia table QA). Analyses
attribute this transferability to the successful mining of prevalent,
highly useful patterns induced from broad trajectories. This challenges
the common assumption that experience is inherently model- and
task-specific and must be managed through the retrieval of episodic
memories
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">ouyang2026reasoningbankscalingagentselfevolving</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">wang2024agentworkflowmemory</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">qian2024investigateconsolidateexploitgeneralstrategyintertask</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">nottingham2024skillsetoptimizationreinforcing</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">liu2025contextualexperiencereplayselfimprovement</span>).
Instead, we show that experience can be distilled into transferable,
declarative skills. We further confirm the effectiveness of Trace2Skill
on creating useful skills for math and vision reasoning.

</div>

<div id="S1.p8" class="ltx_para">

Further analysis shows that Trace2Skill outperforms other popular
paradigms of experience-learning: (1) Reasoning Bank
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">ouyang2026reasoningbankscalingagentselfevolving</span>)
that first saves generalizable lessons from each trajectory, and
retrieve useful experiences at inference time based on task similarity.
(2) An online setting where new trajectories sequentially come in, and
the skill evolves based on new lessons learned. Crucially, because the
skills created or deepened by Trace2Skill operate entirely without an
external retrieval module, they are seamlessly portable across the
broader agent-skill ecosystem.

</div>

<div id="S1.p9" class="ltx_para">

- <span id="S1.I2.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I2.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  Trace2Skill, a framework for automatic skill creation and adaptation
  that supports both deepening existing human-written skills and
  creating new ones from scratch. By utilizing fully parallelized patch
  proposal and conflict-free consolidation, Trace2Skill mirrors human
  skill writing: building broad prior knowledge through extensive
  trajectory analysis before drafting comprehensive skills
  (§<a href="#S2" class="ltx_ref"
  title="2 Trace2Skill ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
  class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>).

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I2.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I2.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  Empirical evidence that trajectory-grounded evolution yields
  high-quality, generalizable skills that transfer effectively across
  LLM scales and out-of-distribution task domains
  (§<a href="#S3" class="ltx_ref"
  title="3 Experiments ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
  class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>).

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I2.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I2.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  A demonstration that open-source, small-scale LLMs (e.g., 35B) are
  sufficient for robust skill evolution, removing the dependency on
  proprietary models seen in concurrent work
  (§<a href="#S3" class="ltx_ref"
  title="3 Experiments ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
  class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>).

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I2.i4"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I2.i4.p1" class="ltx_para">

  Further analysis showing that parallelized outperforms sequential
  online skill updates; single comprehensive skill outperforms
  retrieval-based reasoning banks; and agentic error analysis
  outperforms plain LLM-based analysis (§<a href="#S4" class="ltx_ref"
  title="4 Analysis ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
  class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>).

  </div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2" class="section ltx_section" lang="en">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">2 </span>Trace2Skill

<figure id="S2.F2" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2603.25158/assets/figures/trace2skill_framwork.png"
id="S2.F2.g1" class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape"
width="598" height="216" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S2.F2.15.6.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 2</span>: </span><span
id="S2.F2.10.5" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Overview of
Trace2Skill’s three-stage pipeline. <span id="S2.F2.10.5.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Stage 1:</span> a frozen agent <span
class="math inline"><em>π</em><sub><em>θ</em></sub></span> rolls out on
the evolving set using an initial skill <span
class="math inline">𝒮<sub>0</sub></span> (human-written or LLM-drafted),
producing labeled trajectories <span
class="math inline">𝒯<sup>−</sup></span> (failures) and <span
class="math inline">𝒯<sup>+</sup></span> (successes). <span
id="S2.F2.10.5.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Stage 2:</span>
parallel error and success analysts independently processes individual
traces and proposes skill patches. <span id="S2.F2.10.5.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Stage 3:</span> all patches are merged
into a single consolidated update via inductive reasoning with
programmatic conflict prevention, producing an evolved skill <span
class="math inline">𝒮<sup>*</sup></span> with improved performance and
generalization. </span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Figure <a href="#S2.F2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 2 ‣ 2 Trace2Skill ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> visualizes the three-stage
pipeline of Trace2Skill. We first formalize the skill structure and the
evolution objective (§<a href="#S2.SS1" class="ltx_ref"
title="2.1 Skill and Problem Formalization ‣ 2 Trace2Skill ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2.1</span></a>). Stage 1, Stage 2, and
Stage 3 are detailed in §<a href="#S2.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="2.2 Stage 1: Trajectory Generation ‣ 2 Trace2Skill ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2.2</span></a>,
§<a href="#S2.SS3" class="ltx_ref"
title="2.3 Stage 2: Parallel Multi-Agent Patch Proposal ‣ 2 Trace2Skill ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2.3</span></a>, and
§<a href="#S2.SS4" class="ltx_ref"
title="2.4 Stage 3: Conflict-Free Patch Consolidation ‣ 2 Trace2Skill ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2.4</span></a> accordingly.

</div>

<div id="S2.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.1 </span>Skill and Problem Formalization

<div id="S2.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

A *skill* $`\mathcal{S}`$ is a structured, human-readable knowledge
directory consisting of a root markdown document $`M`$
(<span id="S2.SS1.p1.3.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">SKILL.md</span>) and a set of
auxiliary resources $`\mathcal{R}=\{r_{1},\ldots,r_{K}\}`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\mathcal{S}=(M,\,\mathcal{R}),\quad\mathcal{R}=\{\text{scripts},\,\text{references},\,\text{assets}\}.
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(1)</span> |

$`M`$ encodes procedural knowledge in natural language: when to apply a
technique, step-by-step strategies, and known failure modes. Auxiliary
resources provide executable scripts for deterministic subtasks and
context- or domain-specific references.

</div>

<div id="S2.SS1.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Skill Evolution Problem Formalization.

<div id="S2.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Let $`\pi_{\theta}`$ denote an LLM-based agent with fixed parameters
$`\theta`$, equipped at inference time with a prepended skill
$`\mathcal{S}`$. Let $`\mathcal{D}_{\text{evolve}}`$ and
$`\mathcal{D}_{\text{test}}`$ be disjoint task sets drawn from
potentially different distributions. We define success rate as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S};\pi_{\theta},\mathcal{D})=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}|}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{D}}\mathbf{1}[\pi_{\theta}(t;\mathcal{S})=y_{t}^{*}],
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(2)</span> |

where $`y_{t}^{*}`$ is the ground-truth answer for task $`t`$. The
objective of *skill evolution* is to construct an improved skill from
trajectories on $`\mathcal{D}_{\text{evolve}}`$, without updating
$`\theta`$, such that:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\mathcal{S}^{*}=\mathcal{E}(\mathcal{S}_{0},\mathcal{D}_{\text{evolve}};\pi_{\theta}),\quad\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S}^{*};\,\pi_{\theta},\,\mathcal{D}_{\text{test}})>\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S}_{0};\,\pi_{\theta},\,\mathcal{D}_{\text{test}}).
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(3)</span> |

We study two initializations for $`\mathcal{S}_{0}`$: a
human-expert-written skill (deepening mode) and an LLM-generated draft
from parametric knowledge alone (creation mode), reflecting the two
primary real-world use cases of Trace2Skill.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.2 </span>Stage 1: Trajectory Generation

<div id="S2.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

We adopt ReAct
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">yao2023reactsynergizingreasoningacting</span>)
as the agent harness. Given $`\mathcal{S}_{0}`$, we run $`\pi_{\theta}`$
on each task $`t_{i}\in\mathcal{D}_{\text{evolve}}`$ with query
$`q_{i}`$, yielding a trajectory:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\tau_{i}=\pi_{\theta}(q_{i};\mathcal{S}_{0})=\bigl(q_{i},\;(r_{1}^{(i)},a_{1}^{(i)},o_{1}^{(i)}),\;\ldots,\;(r_{T_{i}}^{(i)},a_{T_{i}}^{(i)},o_{T_{i}}^{(i)}),\;y_{i}\bigr),
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(4)</span> |

where $`r_{k}^{(i)}`$ is the $`k`$-th reasoning trace, $`a_{k}^{(i)}`$
the tool call, $`o_{k}^{(i)}`$ the observation, and $`y_{i}\in\{0,1\}`$
the correctness outcome. The corpus
$`\mathcal{T}=\{\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{N}\}`$ is partitioned into:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\mathcal{T}^{-}=\{\tau_{i}\in\mathcal{T}:y_{i}=0\},\qquad\mathcal{T}^{+}=\{\tau_{i}\in\mathcal{T}:y_{i}=1\}.
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(5)</span> |

Trajectory generation is fully parallelizable; in practice, 200
trajectories with 50+ turns using a 122B-parameter LLM require less than
2 GPU-hours. The agent system prompt template is reproduced in Appendix
<a href="#A2.SS1" class="ltx_ref"
title="B.1 Stage 1: Agent System Prompt Template ‣ Appendix B Prompt Templates and Intermediate Outputs ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">B.1</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.3 </span>Stage 2: Parallel Multi-Agent Patch Proposal

<div id="S2.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

A fleet of specialized analyst sub-agents, each assigned to a *single*
trajectory $`\tau_{i}`$, independently proposes edits to the skill. Each
analyst takes a frozen copy of $`\mathcal{S}_{0}`$ and one trajectory,
and outputs a *skill patch*:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
p_{i}=\begin{cases}\mathcal{A}^{-}(\mathcal{S}_{0},\;\tau_{i}),&\tau_{i}\in\mathcal{T}^{-}\\
\mathcal{A}^{+}(\mathcal{S}_{0},\;\tau_{i}),&\tau_{i}\in\mathcal{T}^{+}\end{cases}
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(6)</span> |

All analysts are dispatched concurrently to a thread pool, yielding the
patch pool $`\mathcal{P}=\{p_{i}\}`$ with no sequential dependency
between agents. Both roles are instructed to propose patches that
generalize beyond the single observed trajectory, and strictly follow
Anthropic’s recommendation for skill writing style
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">anthropic2026skillcreatorconversation</span>)
on conciseness, actionability, and hierarchical disclosure. Since we
assume no stronger teacher model is available, errors are substantially
harder to diagnose than successes, motivating asymmetric analyst
designs.

</div>

<div id="S2.SS3.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Success Analyst ($`\mathcal{A}^{+}`$).

<div id="S2.SS3.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

$`\mathcal{A}^{+}`$ follows a fixed single-pass workflow: it cleans the
trajectory, identifies generalizable behavior patterns that contributed
to the correct answer, and proposes skill patches. The single-call
design is both sufficient and efficient since successful trajectories
require no interactive diagnosis.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS3.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Error Analyst ($`\mathcal{A}^{-}`$).

<div id="S2.SS3.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

$`\mathcal{A}^{-}`$ is implemented as a ReAct-style multi-turn agentic
loop. Given $`\tau_{i}\in\mathcal{T}^{-}`$, it can inspect the full
trace, read input/output files, and compare the agent’s answer against
ground truth — iteratively narrowing down the root cause before
proposing a patch. The loop terminates when $`\mathcal{A}^{-}`$ either
(1) successfully fixes and causally explains the failure, or (2)
exhausts its turn budget. If neither condition yields a valid causal
analysis, $`\tau_{i}`$ is excluded from the patch pool. This quality
gate ensures every patch in $`\mathcal{P}^{-}`$ is grounded in a
verified failure cause, in contrast to prior work deriving insights via
a single non-interactive LLM call
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">ouyang2026reasoningbankscalingagentselfevolving</span>).
An ablation comparing agentic and LLM-only error analysis is presented
in §<a href="#S4.SS3" class="ltx_ref"
title="4.3 Agentic Error Analysis vs. Single-LLM-Call Baselines ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4.3</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS3.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Independence of Patch Proposal.

<div id="S2.SS3.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

All analysts operate on a frozen copy of $`\mathcal{S}_{0}`$ with no
visibility into other agents’ patches. This independence prevents
premature convergence, preserving the full diversity of per-trajectory
observations in $`\mathcal{P}`$. Analyst prompt templates and
representative example patches are provided in Appendix
<a href="#A2.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="B.2 Stage 2: Analyst Prompt Templates and Example Patches ‣ Appendix B Prompt Templates and Intermediate Outputs ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">B.2</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.4 </span>Stage 3: Conflict-Free Patch Consolidation

<div id="S2.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

Let $`\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}^{-}\cup\mathcal{P}^{+}`$ be the full patch
pool from Stage 2. Stage 3 consolidates $`\mathcal{P}`$ into a single
coherent skill update $`p^{*}`$ and applies it to $`\mathcal{S}_{0}`$,
jointly serving two purposes: conflict elimination and inductive
generalization.

</div>

<div id="S2.SS4.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Hierarchical merging with programmatic conflict prevention.

<div id="S2.SS4.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

The patches are merged in a hierarchy of
$`L=\lceil\log_{B_{\text{merge}}}|\mathcal{P}|\rceil`$ levels
($`L\leq L_{\text{max}}`$). At each level $`\ell`$, groups of up to
$`B_{\text{merge}}`$ patches are synthesized into a single consolidated
patch:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
p^{(\ell+1)}=\mathcal{M}\!\left(\pi_{\theta},\;\mathcal{S}_{0},\;\{p_{1}^{(\ell)},\ldots,p_{B_{\text{merge}}}^{(\ell)}\}\right),
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(7)</span> |

where $`\mathcal{M}(\pi_{\theta},\mathcal{S}_{0},\cdot)`$ deduplicates,
resolves conflicts, and preserves unique insights. Crucially,
$`\mathcal{M}`$ reuses the same $`\pi_{\theta}`$ that generated
trajectories and proposed patches — making the entire pipeline
*self-contained*: a single LLM collects experience, analyzes it, and
distills it into an improved skill with no external teacher. The final
$`p^{*}`$ is translated into diff-style edit operations and applied
programmatically. Three deterministic guardrails enforce correctness:
(1) patches referencing non-existent files are rejected; (2) edits
targeting the same line range within the same file are flagged as
conflicts and withheld; (3) the updated $`\mathcal{S}`$ is validated by
a skill format checker.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS4.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Patch consolidation as inductive reasoning.

<div id="S2.SS4.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Beyond conflict elimination, the hierarchical application of
$`\mathcal{M}`$ performs *inductive reasoning* over the patch pool.
Because each $`p_{i}`$ derives from a single trajectory, $`\mathcal{P}`$
as a whole encodes the distribution of behaviors $`\pi_{\theta}`$
exhibits across the evolving set. $`\mathcal{M}`$ is explicitly
instructed to identify *prevalent patterns* — edits appearing
consistently across independent patches — on the grounds that recurring
observations across diverse trajectories are more likely to reflect
systematic task properties and generalize to unseen tasks and different
agent models. Conversely, edits appearing in only one or few patches are
treated as potentially idiosyncratic and discarded. This
prevalence-weighted consolidation is the mechanism by which deep
per-trajectory analysis produces a generalizable skill.

</div>

<div id="S2.SS4.SSS0.Px2.p2" class="ltx_para">

The evolved skill $`\mathcal{S}^{*}=(M^{*},\mathcal{R}^{*})`$ replaces
$`\mathcal{S}_{0}`$ and is used directly at inference without any
retrieval index. The merge operator prompt template and an example
consolidated patch $`p^{*}`$ are given in Appendix
<a href="#A2.SS3" class="ltx_ref"
title="B.3 Stage 3: Merge Operator Prompt and Example Consolidated Patch ‣ Appendix B Prompt Templates and Intermediate Outputs ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">B.3</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS5" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.5 </span>Two Evolution Modes

<div id="S2.SS5.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Skill deepening.

<div id="S2.SS5.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

$`\mathcal{S}_{0}`$ is initialized with a human-expert-written skill.
The pipeline refines $`\mathcal{S}_{0}`$ by adding failure-specific
guidance from $`\mathcal{T}^{-}`$ and reinforcing effective strategies
from $`\mathcal{T}^{+}`$.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS5.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Skill creation from scratch.

<div id="S2.SS5.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

$`\mathcal{S}_{0}`$ is initialized with a skill drafted by
$`\pi_{\theta}`$ from parametric knowledge alone, with no access to task
trajectories. As we show in §<a href="#S3" class="ltx_ref"
title="3 Experiments ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>, this draft provides no
substantial improvement over no skill —
$`\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S}_{0};\pi_{\theta},\mathcal{D}_{\text{test}})\approx\mathcal{P}(\emptyset;\pi_{\theta},\mathcal{D}_{\text{test}})`$
— so evolution from this point constitutes genuine skill creation: the
pipeline produces a useful skill from a performance-neutral
initialization, driven entirely by trajectory evidence.

</div>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3" class="section ltx_section" lang="en">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">3 </span>Experiments

<div id="S3.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.1 </span>Experimental Setup

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Datasets and Skills.

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Our main experiments focus on the spreadsheet domain, which challenges
agents to interact with a file system and manipulate
<span id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">xlsx</span> files whose contents
are hard to inspect without structured tooling. We use
SpreadsheetBench-Verified
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">ma2024spreadsheetbenchchallengingrealworld</span>),
splitting its 400 samples into 200 for the *evolving set* and 200
held-out for testing; no test samples are seen during evolution. We
additionally report Soft (sub-problem pass rate) and Hard (all
sub-problems must pass) scores on the full SpreadsheetBench. For
out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization, we evaluate on
WikiTableQuestions
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">pasupat2015compositionalsemanticparsingsemistructured</span>)
(WikiTQ), which differs in data source (Wikipedia) and task type
(compositional semantic parsing); inputs and expected outputs are
converted to spreadsheet format so the <span id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">xlsx</span> skill applies without
modification. All results are averaged over three random seeds (41, 42,
43) using each benchmark’s official evaluation criteria.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2" class="ltx_para">

Two baseline skills are compared: (1) the Anthropic official
<span id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">xlsx</span> skill
(<span id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Human-Written</span>), a high-quality
human-expert-written skill; and (2) an <span id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">xlsx-basic</span> skill generated
by prompting Qwen3.5-122B-A10B from parametric knowledge alone
(<span id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Parametric</span>), containing only
common-sense-level task descriptions with no trajectory grounding
(details in Appendix <a href="#A2.SS1" class="ltx_ref"
title="B.1 Stage 1: Agent System Prompt Template ‣ Appendix B Prompt Templates and Intermediate Outputs ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">B.1</span></a>).

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Skill Settings.

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

We evaluate six conditions: <span id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">No Skill</span> (no skill document),
<span id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Human-Written</span>
(<span id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">xlsx</span>),
<span id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Parametric</span>
(<span id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">xlsx-basic</span>),
<span id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.6"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">+Error</span> (Trace2Skill with error
analysts only), <span id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.7"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">+Success</span> (Trace2Skill with success
analysts only), and <span id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.8"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">+Combined</span> (Trace2Skill with both
analyst types). <span id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.9"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Skill Deepening</span> initializes from
Human-Written; <span id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.10"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Skill Creation</span> initializes from
Parametric.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Implementation Details.

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Trace2Skill conducts end-to-end self-evolution: the same LLM serves as
trajectory generator, patch proposer, and skill editor. We experiment
with two Qwen3.5 MoE models: Qwen3.5-122B-A10B and Qwen3.5-35B-A3B. Both
are instruct/think hybrid models; we use instruct mode for multi-turn
ReAct-style agentic tasks and thinking mode for single-call tasks
(hierarchical merging, success analysis, patch conversion). Models are
served with vLLM
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">kwon2023efficientmemorymanagementlarge</span>)
using the recommended Qwen3.5 generation
configuration<span id="footnote2"
class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup>2</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>2</sup><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">2</span>See
<a href="https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3.5-35B-A3B"
class="ltx_ref ltx_url ltx_font_typewriter"
target="_blank">https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3.5-35B-A3B</a> and
<a href="https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3.5-122B-A10B"
class="ltx_ref ltx_url ltx_font_typewriter"
target="_blank">https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3.5-122B-A10B</a>.</span></span></span>.
Stage 1 generates 1 trajectory per problem. At Stage 2, 128 sub-agents
run in parallel, and we use a merge batch size of 32. For all
ReAct-style agents, we set the interaction turn budget to 100.

</div>

id="S3.T1.22.2" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Main results
shown as deltas (%). <span id="S3.T1.22.2.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Skill Author</span> = model that evolved
the skill (row groups); <span id="S3.T1.22.2.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Skill User</span> = model at inference
(column groups). Reference rows remain absolute scores for context.
Evolved rows show signed deltas with per-column color intensity; <span
id="S3.T1.22.2.3" class="ltx_text"
style="--ltx-bg-color:#80E680;">green</span> = improvement, <span
id="S3.T1.22.2.4" class="ltx_text"
style="--ltx-bg-color:#FF8080;">red</span> = decline. <span
id="S3.T1.22.2.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Deltas:</span> <span

measured against the <span id="S3.T1.22.2.7"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Human-Written</span> baseline; <span
id="S3.T1.22.2.8" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">Creation</span>
against the <span id="S3.T1.22.2.9"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Parametric</span> baseline. <span
id="S3.T1.22.2.10" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Avg</span>: equally
weights in-distribution SpreadsheetBench (Vrf/Soft/Hard, both model
scales) and OOD WikiTQ (both model scales), expressed as delta from the
corresponding baseline.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.2 </span>Main Results

<div id="S3.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Table <a href="#S3.T1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 1 ‣ Implementation Details. ‣ 3.1 Experimental Setup ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a> presents results across all
skill conditions, model scales, and transfer directions. We report the
performance of evolved skills as deltas against their respective
baselines: comparing skill deepening against existing human-written
skills, and skill creation against the model’s base parametric
performance. We use <span id="S3.SS2.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Avg</span> as the primary summary metric:
a skill that genuinely benefits an agent should transfer across model
scales *and* task domains, so Avg equally weights in-distribution
SpreadsheetBench performance (Vrf/Soft/Hard, both model scales) and
WikiTQ transfer performance (both model scales), rewarding
generalization rather than in-distribution specialization.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Human-written is a strong handcrafted prior, but not a portable one; parametric is weak.

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

The Human-Written baseline is strong for the 122B agent, reaching 48.33%
on SprBench-Vrf and 74.68% on WikiTQ, but it does not transfer cleanly
across model scale: for the 35B agent it underperforms No Skill by
$`-`$<!-- -->9.3 pp on SprBench-Vrf and $`-`$<!-- -->4.3 pp on WikiTQ.
By contrast, the Parametric baseline remains close to No Skill overall
(26.17% vs. 27.67% SprBench-Vrf for the 122B agent), confirming that
parametric knowledge alone does not yield useful skill content
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">han2026sweskillsbenchagentskillsactually</span>).
These two references motivate both Deepening and Creation: the former
asks whether a strong manual prior can be refined, while the latter asks
whether trajectory-grounded distillation can build a useful skill
starting from an inadequate one.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Deepening reliably strengthens the human-written skill on in-distribution spreadsheet tasks.

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Starting from Human-Written, 122B-authored Deepening gains
$`+`$<!-- -->17.5 pp on SprBench-Vrf with +Error and $`+`$<!-- -->21.5
pp with +Combined, while also improving Soft and Hard scores. These
gains are not confined to the authoring model: the 35B-authored
Deepening +Error skill gains $`+`$<!-- -->16.7 pp on SprBench-Vrf when
used by the 122B agent, and the 122B-authored counterpart gains
$`+`$<!-- -->27.0 pp for the 35B agent. The same refined skills also
transfer beyond the training distribution, with 122B-authored Deepening
improving WikiTQ by $`+`$<!-- -->1.6 pp (+Error) and $`+`$<!-- -->4.6 pp
(+Combined).

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Creation substantially outperforms the weak parametric baseline and can match or exceed human-written quality.

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Because Parametric is a poor starting point, the relevant comparison is
whether distilled skills recover meaningful capability from scratch. The
answer is yes: 122B-authored Creation +Error gains $`+`$<!-- -->22.8 pp
on SprBench-Vrf, bringing performance close to Human-Written despite
starting from a weak prior. In some settings Creation even outperforms
Human-Written: the 35B-authored Creation +Error skill, when used by the
122B agent, gains $`+`$<!-- -->57.7 pp on WikiTQ over Parametric
(<span id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px3.p1.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">best
in table</span>, reaching 81.38%) and surpasses Human-Written by
$`+`$<!-- -->6.7 pp.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px4" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### The Avg column identifies the settings that are robust across in-distribution, OOD, and cross-model use.

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px4.p1" class="ltx_para">

Viewed through Avg, the strongest configurations are those that improve
multiple slices of the table at once rather than spiking on only one
benchmark or skill user model. The best Avg scores come from Creation,
with 35B-authored Creation +Error reaching $`+`$<!-- -->18.3 pp and
122B-authored Creation +Success reaching $`+`$<!-- -->17.6 pp, showing
that from-scratch skill synthesis can remain strong after averaging over
both datasets and both user models. At the same time, Deepening remains
broadly competitive, and +Combined is noteworthy because it stays
consistently high across all four Author–Mode combinations rather than
depending on a single especially favorable setting.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px5" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Across analyst types, +Combined is the most consistently strong signal, +Error the most reliable, and +Success the most volatile.

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px5.p1" class="ltx_para">

Measured by Avg improvement over the corresponding reference baseline,
<span id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px5.p1.2.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">+Combined</span> is the steadiest high
performer, while <span id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px5.p1.2.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">+Error</span> remains reliably positive
in every setting and serves as the safest default signal.
<span id="S3.SS2.SSS0.Px5.p1.2.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">+Success</span>, by contrast, has the
highest variance: it produces the largest single-setting Avg gain
($`+`$<!-- -->17.6 pp for 122B-authored Creation) but is also the only
condition that drops below baseline ($`-`$<!-- -->0.9 pp for
122B-authored Deepening). This pattern suggests that success-derived
patches can be highly valuable, but only when the hierarchical merge
filters them effectively; otherwise they are less stable than
error-driven updates, motivating a more selective success analyst design
(see §<a href="#S6" class="ltx_ref"
title="6 Conclusion ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a>).

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

reasoning results shown as deltas from the No Skill baseline. <span
id="S3.T2.13.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">D-Test</span>:
DAPO-Math-Test-100 pass rate (%); <span id="S3.T2.14.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">AIME</span>: AIME 2026 avg@8 over 30
problems (%). Reference row remains absolute; evolved rows use <span
id="S3.T2.15.3" class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#B3F0B3;"> green
/ <span id="S3.T2.15.3.1" class="ltx_text"
style="--ltx-bg-color:#FFB3B3;"> red delta intensity.
</span></span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S3.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

To assess whether Trace2Skill generalizes beyond spreadsheets, we apply
it to mathematical reasoning using <span id="S3.SS3.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">DAPO-Math-Train-400</span> as the
evolving set.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

We evaluate on <span id="S3.SS3.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">DAPO-Math-Test-100</span>
(in-distribution; pass rate %) and <span id="S3.SS3.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">AIME 2026</span> (out-of-distribution
competition mathematics; avg@8 over 30 problems). Following the
cross-model protocol of Section <a href="#S3.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="3.2 Main Results ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.2</span></a>, we create skills from
scratch using error analysts. Results are shown in Table
<a href="#S3.T2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 2 ‣ 3.3 Math Reasoning ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS3.p3" class="ltx_para">

Distilled math skills yield consistent gains across models and
benchmarks (Table <a href="#S3.T2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 2 ‣ 3.3 Math Reasoning ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>). The 122B-authored skill
gains $`+`$<!-- -->3.0 pp on DAPO-Math-Test-100 and $`+`$<!-- -->2.9 pp
on AIME 2026 (same-model), and transfers positively cross-model:
$`+`$<!-- -->5.0 pp on DAPO-Math-Test-100 and $`+`$<!-- -->5.0 pp on
AIME 2026 for the 35B agent. The 35B-authored skill is comparably
effective: $`+`$<!-- -->4.0 pp same-model and $`+`$<!-- -->2.0 pp
cross-model on DAPO-Math-Test-100, confirming that trajectory-grounded
distillation is domain-agnostic and scales to competition-level
evaluation.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.4 </span>Visual Question Answering

<div id="S3.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

To evaluate whether Trace2Skill generalizes to multimodal visual
reasoning, we apply it to <span id="S3.SS4.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Visual Question Answering (VQA)</span>
using <span id="S3.SS4.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">DocVQA</span>
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">mathew2020docvqa</span>
as the target benchmark. DocVQA requires jointly understanding document
images—forms, tables, invoices, letters, reports—and answering
natural-language questions by extracting, locating, and reasoning over
visual and textual elements. We use the official validation split (5,349
question–image pairs), reserving the first 2,700 instances as the
*evolving set* and the remaining 2,649 as the held-out *evaluation set*.
We report <span id="S3.SS4.p1.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">ANLS</span> (Average Normalized
Levenshtein Similarity, the official metric) and
<span id="S3.SS4.p1.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Accuracy</span>
(ANLS $`\geq 0.5`$, %). Similar to Section
<a href="#S3.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="3.2 Main Results ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.2</span></a>, we create skills from
scratch using error analysts. Results are shown in Table
<a href="#S3.T3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 3 ‣ 3.4 Visual Question Answering ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>.

</div>

<figure id="S3.T3" class="ltx_table ltx_align_floatright">
<table id="S3.T3.6" class="ltx_tabular ltx_centering ltx_align_middle">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 3: </span>DocVQA
results shown as deltas from the No Skill baseline (evaluation set:
2,649 instances). <span id="S3.T3.16.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">ANLS</span>: Average Normalized
Levenshtein Similarity; <span id="S3.T3.17.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Acc</span>: ANLS <span
class="math inline"> ≥ 0.5</span> (%). Reference row remains absolute;
evolved rows use <span id="S3.T3.18.3" class="ltx_text"
style="--ltx-bg-color:#B3F0B3;"> green / <span id="S3.T3.18.3.1"
class="ltx_text" style="--ltx-bg-color:#FFB3B3;"> red delta intensity.
</span></span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S3.SS4.p2" class="ltx_para">

Table <a href="#S3.T3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 3 ‣ 3.4 Visual Question Answering ‣ 3 Experiments ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a> presents a nuanced picture.
The No Skill row reveals an unexpected reversal: the 35B agent (0.6843
ANLS) outperforms the 122B agent (0.6424 ANLS) on DocVQA without any
skill.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS4.p3" class="ltx_para">

Despite this task performance advantage, the 35B model falls behind as a
skill *author*. The 35B-authored skill yields negligible gains for the
122B model ($`+`$<!-- -->0.009 ANLS) and actively degrades same-model
35B performance ($`-`$<!-- -->0.062 ANLS, $`-`$<!-- -->6.2 pp accuracy).
By contrast, the 122B-authored skill gains $`+`$<!-- -->0.1639 ANLS and
$`+`$<!-- -->15.3 pp accuracy (same-model), and transfers just as
strongly to the 35B model ($`+`$<!-- -->0.1554 ANLS, $`+`$<!-- -->13.6
pp accuracy). This dissociation suggests that inductive reasoning for
skill authoring — identifying recurring failure patterns across
trajectories and distilling them into actionable rules — is a distinct
capability from task execution. A model that performs well on DocVQA
does not necessarily possess the reflective capacity to analyze *why* it
fails and generalize those observations into a transferable skill.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4" class="section ltx_section" lang="en">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">4 </span>Analysis

<div id="S4.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.1 </span>Parallel Consolidation vs. Sequential Editing

<div id="S4.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

In the online skill evolution paradigm, the skill is updated
sequentially as new trajectory batches arrive. We isolate the
contribution of parallel, many-to-one consolidation by comparing against
two sequential baselines: <span id="S4.SS1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Seq-$`B{=}1`$</span>, where the skill is
updated after every single trajectory, and <span id="S4.SS1.p1.2.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Seq-$`B{=}4`$</span>, where the skill is
updated after every batch of four trajectories. All three conditions use
error analysts only and initialize from the Human-Written skill; results
are shown in Table <a href="#S4.T4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 4 ‣ 4.1 Parallel Consolidation vs. Sequential Editing ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>.

</div>

Deepening, +Error only, %). Seq-<span
class="math inline"><em>B</em></span>: skill updated after every batch
of <span class="math inline"><em>B</em></span> trajectories. <span
id="S4.T4.24.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Bold</span> = best per
column.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

On the 122B model, parallel consolidation outperforms both sequential
settings across all SpreadsheetBench metrics ($`+`$<!-- -->4.0 pp Vrf
over Seq-$`B{=}1`$, $`+`$<!-- -->6.8 pp over Seq-$`B{=}4`$). On the 35B
model, parallel wins on Vrf but Seq-$`B{=}1`$ scores modestly higher on
Soft and Hard, suggesting the smaller model may benefit from more
incremental updates in some conditions. However, this marginal quality
variation comes at 20$`\times`$ the computational cost, and the
efficiency gap widens linearly with the number of trajectories analyzed.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Latency.

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

With $`W{=}128`$ workers and $`N{\approx}70`$ error lessons, all
analysts execute in a single parallel round; the hierarchical merge adds
$`\lceil\log_{2}N\rceil{\approx}7`$ further sequential rounds (one per
merge layer), yielding $`{\approx}8`$ sequential LLM-call rounds in
total. The sequential baselines require $`N`$ and $`\lceil N/B\rceil`$
rounds respectively, since each skill edit depends on the preceding one.
In practice this translates to 3 min (parallel) vs. 60 min
(Seq-$`B{=}1`$, $`20{\times}`$) and 15 min (Seq-$`B{=}4`$,
$`5{\times}`$), with the gap scaling linearly in $`N`$. All times are in
node hours of an 8-GPU A800 node.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p2" class="ltx_para">

Beyond efficiency, parallel consolidation has a structural advantage:
all patch proposals are derived from the same frozen initial skill
$`\mathcal{S}_{0}`$, preventing the *sequential drift* inherent to the
online setting, where each skill update alters the context in which
subsequent trajectories are analyzed. The hierarchical merge then
performs inductive reasoning over the full population of
trajectory-local observations simultaneously, selecting patterns that
recur across diverse trajectories rather than patterns that recur in the
most recent updates.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.2 </span>Trace2Skill vs. Retrieval-Memory Baseline

<div id="S4.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

ReasoningBank
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">ouyang2026reasoningbankscalingagentselfevolving</span>)
stores generalizable lessons derived from each trajectory and retrieves
the most relevant memories at inference time using task-query
similarity. Since it draws on both success and failure trajectories, we
compare it against <span id="S4.SS2.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">+Combined</span>, which uses the same
trajectory pool but distills it into a portable skill document rather
than maintaining a retrieval index. We implement a ReasoningBank-style
retrieval baseline with their official prompt and recommended retrieval
setting (top 1) with Qwen3-Embedding-8B as the retriever. Results on
same-model Deepening are shown in Table <a href="#S4.T5" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 5 ‣ 4.2 Trace2Skill vs. Retrieval-Memory Baseline ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a>.

</div>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 5:
</span>Trace2Skill (Human Written+Combined) vs. ReasoningBank on
SpreadsheetBench (same-model Deepening, %). ReasoningBank retrieves
success and failure memories at inference via Qwen3-Embedding-8B;
+Combined distills the same trajectory pool into a single portable skill
with no retrieval module. <span id="S4.T5.12.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Bold</span> = best per
column.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

+Combined outperforms ReasoningBank by large margins: $`+`$<!-- -->13.8
pp Vrf, $`+`$<!-- -->7.1 pp Soft, $`+`$<!-- -->8.2 pp Hard for the 122B
model; $`+`$<!-- -->9.2 pp Vrf, $`+`$<!-- -->1.5 pp Soft,
$`+`$<!-- -->0.8 pp Hard for 35B. ReasoningBank on the 35B model (20.50%
Vrf) barely exceeds No Skill (19.00%), indicating the retriever fails to
surface relevant guidance when the 35B model’s query representations do
not align well with the stored memory embeddings.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

We attribute the performance gap to three factors. First, retrieval
quality is sensitive to surface-level similarity between the test query
and stored memory keys: when the test distribution differs in phrasing
or structure from the evolving set, retrieval degrades, while the
distilled skill transfers without modification. Second, retrieved
snippets compete with the task context for model attention, whereas a
skill pre-loaded into the system prompt is already integrated before any
task-specific content is seen. Third, Trace2Skill’s hierarchical merge
actively deduplicates and abstracts trajectory-local observations into
general principles; raw trajectory summaries in a retrieval bank are not
filtered for redundancy or generalizability. Taken together, these
results support the premise that distillation into a compact,
model-agnostic skill document is a more effective use of trajectory
evidence than episodic retrieval.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.3 </span>Agentic Error Analysis vs. Single-LLM-Call Baselines

<div id="S4.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Many prior works derive transferable lessons or skills from error
trajectories via a single non-interactive LLM call
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">ouyang2026reasoningbankscalingagentselfevolving</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">xia2026skillrlevolvingagentsrecursive</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">yang2026autoskillexperiencedrivenlifelonglearning</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">jiang2026xskillcontinuallearningexperience</span>).
The <span id="S4.SS3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">+Error
LLM</span> condition ablates our agentic loop design: a single LLM call
receives each error trajectory and proposes a patch directly, without
the ability to inspect files, query ground truth, or iteratively narrow
the root cause. Table <a href="#S4.T6" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 6 ‣ 4.3 Agentic Error Analysis vs. Single-LLM-Call Baselines ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a> compares +Error (agentic,
ours) against +Error LLM across all four Author–Mode combinations.

</div>

id="S4.T6.7.17.6.1" class="ltx_text"
style="font-size:90%;">23.50</span></td>
<td id="S4.T6.7.17.7" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_bb"
style="padding-left: 3.5pt; padding-right: 3.5pt"><span
id="S4.T6.7.17.7.1" class="ltx_text"
style="font-size:90%;">16.87</span></td>
<td id="S4.T6.7.17.8" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_bb"
style="padding-left: 3.5pt; padding-right: 3.5pt"><span
id="S4.T6.7.17.8.1" class="ltx_text"
style="font-size:90%;">4.93</span></td>
<td id="S4.T6.7.17.9" class="ltx_td ltx_align_center ltx_border_bb"
style="padding-left: 3.5pt; padding-right: 3.5pt"><span
id="S4.T6.7.17.9.1" class="ltx_text"

class="ltx_td ltx_nopad_r ltx_align_center ltx_border_bb"
