</table>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 6: </span>Agentic
error analysis (+Error) vs. single-LLM-call error analysis (+Error LLM)
across all Author–Mode combinations (%). Same-model cells are shaded;
plain cells are cross-model transfer. <span id="S4.T6.6.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Bold</span> = better of the two
conditions per cell.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Agentic analysis wins in Avg across all settings.

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Agentic +Error outperforms +Error LLM in weighted Avg in all four
settings, with gaps of $`+`$<!-- -->12.2 pp (122B Deepening),
$`+`$<!-- -->0.8 pp (122B Creation), $`+`$<!-- -->3.2 pp (35B
Deepening), and $`+`$<!-- -->13.3 pp (35B Creation). The 122B Creation
setting is the only near-tie: both conditions score around 27 pp,
masking a striking internal divergence (see below).

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Agentic error analysis produces more transferable patches.

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

The Avg advantage holds across both in-distribution SpreadsheetBench and
OOD WikiTQ columns: +Error LLM patches that achieve comparable ID scores
frequently degrade on WikiTQ and cross-model cells, while agentic
patches remain positive across both axes.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Why the agentic loop produces more transferable patches.

<div id="S4.SS3.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

A qualitative study of 33 shared error cases confirms the structural
advantage built into $`\mathcal{A}^{-}`$ (§<a href="#S2" class="ltx_ref"
title="2 Trace2Skill ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>): the two pipelines reach
strong agreement on only 4 cases (12.1%), with clear disagreement in 18
(54.5%). The LLM-only analyzer, limited to the execution log,
over-attributes parse failures as the primary root cause in 57% of cases
where parse-error messages appear (vs. 14% for agentic), and in at least
one case hallucinated three distinct failure causes for a trajectory
where artifact evaluation confirmed the output was already correct.
Artifact access and fix validation let $`\mathcal{A}^{-}`$ reject such
false positives and anchor each patch to a verified failure mechanism —
producing the domain-general guardrails that transfer across model
scales and OOD settings.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.4 </span>Generalizable SoPs Learned

<div id="S4.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

We inspect the 323 map patches produced by the 122B Deepening +Combined
run to characterize which standard operating procedures (SoPs) the
pipeline distills. The four most prevalent themes together account for
546/323 patch citations (patches can cite multiple themes) and are
encoded directly in the main <span id="S4.SS4.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">SKILL.md</span>; less common
patterns are detailed in Appendix <a href="#A1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix A Secondary SoPs from Qualitative Analysis ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A</span></a>.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS4.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Formula recalculation and write-back verification (178/323 patches).

<div id="S4.SS4.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Run <span id="S4.SS4.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">recalc.py</span> after every
formula write and reopen with <span id="S4.SS4.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">data_only=True</span> to confirm
evaluation; skipping this step leaves cells stale and is the single most
common error mode in the run.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS4.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Tool selection: <span id="S4.SS4.SSS0.Px2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">openpyxl</span> over <span id="S4.SS4.SSS0.Px2.2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">pandas.to_excel()</span> (177/323 patches).

<div id="S4.SS4.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Use <span id="S4.SS4.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">pandas</span> for read/transform
logic and <span id="S4.SS4.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">openpyxl</span> for write-back;
copy the input file to the output path first to preserve all structural
anchors. <span id="S4.SS4.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">pandas.to_excel()</span> silently
destroys formula relationships and named ranges.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS4.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Explicit read-back verification (138/323 patches).

<div id="S4.SS4.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

After writing, reopen the output file and confirm every target cell
holds the expected value before submitting; error trajectories that fail
characteristically omit this check.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS4.SSS0.Px4" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Structural-edit safety (53/323 patches).

<div id="S4.SS4.SSS0.Px4.p1" class="ltx_para">

Delete rows in descending order to prevent index-shift corruption; copy
the input workbook before editing to preserve formatting and formulas.
Error trajectories document both failure modes; success trajectories
confirm the protective workflow.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4.SS4.SSS0.Px5" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Niche quirks are routed to <span id="S4.SS4.SSS0.Px5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">references/</span>.

<div id="S4.SS4.SSS0.Px5.p1" class="ltx_para">

Low-support observations are not discarded but routed into 13
supplementary reference files rather than the main
<span id="S4.SS4.SSS0.Px5.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">SKILL.md</span>. For example, cell
color extraction, FIFO vs LIFO mismatch under a special business logic
etc. This mirrors established skill-design practice: procedural guidance
flows from general to case-specific, with the main document encoding
universal workflow rules and <span id="S4.SS4.SSS0.Px5.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">references/</span> serving as an
on-demand look-up layer for infrequent edge cases. Trace2Skill recovers
this hierarchy automatically from trajectory evidence rather than
requiring manual curation.

</div>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5" class="section ltx_section" lang="en">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">5 </span>Related Work

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Agent Skills.

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Anthropic’s skill framework formalizes skills as lightweight,
expert-authored documents that encode standard operating procedures
(SOPs) for focused task domains. These are designed for dynamic loading,
progressive disclosure, and compatibility with diverse agent harnesses
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">anthropic2026skills</span>).
SkillsBench
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">li2026skillsbenchbenchmarkingagentskills</span>)
provides the first systematic benchmark of skill quality, revealing that
while curated, human-written skills generally improve performance,
self-generated skills relying purely on parametric knowledge rarely
help. Furthermore, they find that a small set of focused skills
consistently outperforms a single, bloated document. Similarly,
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">li2026singleagentskillsreplacemultiagent</span>
demonstrate that single agents augmented with in-depth skills can match
the performance of multi-agent frameworks, though skill retrieval
remains a bottleneck. SWE-Skills-Bench
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">han2026sweskillsbenchagentskillsactually</span>)
evaluates skill injection in real software-engineering tasks, reporting
an average $`+`$<!-- -->1.2% gain when skills are well-matched to the
task context, but a notable performance drop during context mismatch.
AgentSkillOS and SkillNet extend this ecosystem to encompass skill
selection and governance
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">li2026organizingorchestratingbenchmarkingagent</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">liang2026skillnetcreateevaluateconnect</span>).
*Our position:* We build upon the established consensus that
high-quality, focused skills are critical. However, we address a
narrower, underexplored question: given a single skill of bounded scope,
how much can systematic trajectory analysis improve it? The
vulnerability to context mismatch highlighted by SWE-Skills-Bench
directly motivates our design choice to inductively distill
generalizable patterns rather than overfit to specific queries.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Experience Memory for Agent Self-Evolution.

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Early work demonstrated that iterative feedback on execution
trajectories can significantly improve agent behavior. For instance,
Voyager
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">wang2023voyageropenendedembodiedagent</span>)
accumulates reusable skills through open-ended interaction, while
Reflexion
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">shinn2023reflexionlanguageagentsverbal</span>)
refines decisions via verbal self-reflection on past successes and
failures. Building on these foundations, subsequent research has focused
on storing trajectory-derived insights in retrieval banks, which are
then queried to augment future tasks
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">ouyang2026reasoningbankscalingagentselfevolving</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">fang2026mempexploringagentprocedural</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">wang2024agentworkflowmemory</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">qian2024investigateconsolidateexploitgeneralstrategyintertask</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">nottingham2024skillsetoptimizationreinforcing</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">liu2025contextualexperiencereplayselfimprovement</span>).
*Our position:* While these systems rely on test-time retrieval from
episodic memory banks, we explore a fundamentally different approach:
distilling experience into declarative skills as static, shareable
artifacts. This distinction is motivated by two properties. First,
inductive compression across many trajectories smooths out the quirks of
any single episode, yielding robust principles rather than localized
anecdotes. Second, a distilled declarative skill is
architecture-agnostic and seamlessly shareable across agents and model
scales, whereas retrieval banks are typically tightly coupled to the
specific harness that generated them.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Automatic Skill Self-Evolution.

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

The closest neighbors to our work automatically evolve skills from agent
trajectories. SkillWeaver
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">zheng2025skillweaverwebagentsselfimprove</span>)
generates web API skills through structured exploration. AutoSkill
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">yang2026autoskillexperiencedrivenlifelonglearning</span>)
creates and updates skills online from user chat trajectories via an
extraction–maintenance–reuse lifecycle. XSkill
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">jiang2026xskillcontinuallearningexperience</span>)
maintains dual stores: skills encoding task-level SOPs, and experiences
encoding context-sensitive, action-level guidance. EvoSkill
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">alzubi2026evoskillautomatedskilldiscovery</span>)
iteratively diagnoses failures and validates skill updates, making it
the closest single-system neighbor. Memento-Skills
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">zhou2026mementoskillsletagentsdesign</span>)
employs stateful markdown skills updated incrementally through a
read–write loop. Beyond fully automated evolution, Anthropic’s
skill-creator system
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">anthropic2026skillcreatorconversation</span>)
represents the state-of-the-art in human-guided refinement, where
practitioners qualitatively revise skills based on agent outputs from a
small test set. *Our position:* While prior work has actively explored
trajectory-based skill evolution, Trace2Skill introduces three critical
differentiators. (1) <span id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Many-to-one consolidation:</span> We
merge all trajectory-local patches simultaneously rather than editing
the skill sequentially per trajectory, avoiding order dependence and
overfitting to early observations. (2) <span id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Comprehensive declarative
artifacts:</span> We target unified, Anthropic-style skill directories
rather than narrow API objects, dual stores, or retrieval-augmented
hybrids. (3) <span id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">No test-time retrieval:</span> The
evolved skill is consumed directly, making it natively compatible with
any agent harness. Furthermore, while concurrent automated systems rely
on proprietary LLMs (e.g., Claude), our full pipeline achieves robust
evolution using open-source models as small as 35B parameters. Finally,
Trace2Skill serves as a scalable complement to manual oversight (like
Anthropic’s skill-creator), distilling lessons across hundreds of
trajectories where human review would bottleneck.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px4" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Skill and Policy Co-evolution.

<div id="S5.SS0.SSS0.Px4.p1" class="ltx_para">

SkillRL
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">xia2026skillrlevolvingagentsrecursive</span>)
co-evolves skills and model policies via reinforcement learning,
treating skills as localized experience triggers (“when X, do Y”) rather
than comprehensive SOPs. Similarly, ARISE and MetaClaw
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">xia2026metaclawjusttalk</span>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">li2026ariseagentreasoningintrinsic</span>)
explore dual-timescale online adaptation with continual policy updates.
*Our position:* In contrast to co-evolution methods that require
parameter updates, we strictly study frozen-model, training-free,
artifact-level adaptation, ensuring our distilled skills remain entirely
model-agnostic.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6" class="section ltx_section" lang="en">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">6 </span>Conclusion

<div id="S6.p1" class="ltx_para">

We introduced Trace2Skill, a framework for automatic skill creation and
adaptation that simulates how human experts author skills: accumulating
broad domain knowledge through extensive experience before instantiating
it into a concise, declarative artifact. Rather than updating a skill
sequentially as individual trajectories arrive, Trace2Skill dispatches a
parallel fleet of analyst sub-agents to propose targeted editing patches
from disjoint trajectory batches. It then consolidates all proposals
simultaneously into a single, coherent skill directory via inductive
reasoning and programmatic conflict prevention.

</div>

<div id="S6.p2" class="ltx_para">

Our findings show that skills distilled from one model’s trajectories
generalize remarkably well across model scales and to
out-of-distribution tasks. Furthermore, analyses demonstrate that
consolidating a broad set of trajectory-local lessons simultaneously
improves both computational efficiency and downstream performance, and a
single portable skill folder outperforms retrieval-based per-case
experience. By structuring the output as a hierarchical directory (e.g.,
broad principles in a primary ‘SKILL.md‘ file and case-specific
heuristics in a ‘references/‘ subdirectory), Trace2Skill successfully
encodes both generalizable patterns and nuanced, case-specific pitfalls.

</div>

<div id="S6.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Limitation and Future Work.

<div id="S6.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

As a work in progress, the current paper is limited in these aspects,
which we plan to address in the near future: (1)
<span id="S6.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Causal
effect quantification of editing patches:</span> Currently, patches are
consolidated holistically, making it difficult to isolate the marginal
contribution or potential interference of any single proposed change. We
aim to develop methods to rigorously quantify the causal impact of
individual trajectory-derived patches on the final skill. (2)
<span id="S6.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Tracing
the utility of specific skill sections:</span> We have not yet
implemented a mechanism to dynamically trace how heavily the agent
relies on specific sections of the generated skill directory during
inference. Future work will focus on fine-grained attribution tracking
to determine the exact utility of different components (e.g., specific
checklist items vs. reference files), which will enable automated
pruning of ineffective or distracting skill sections.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="bib" class="section ltx_bibliography" lang="en">

## References

</div>

<div class="ltx_pagination ltx_role_newpage">

</div>

<div id="A1" class="section ltx_appendix" lang="en">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix A </span>Secondary SoPs from Qualitative Analysis

<div id="A1.p1" class="ltx_para">

The following SoPs appear in the 122B Deepening +Combined run with
moderate support (10–15/323 patches) and are encoded in the evolved
skill but not discussed in the main text.

</div>

<div id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Target-range and answer-position validation (15/323 patches).

<div id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Before writing, verify the exact target sheet name, cell range, and
<span id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">answer_position</span> field from
the task metadata. Misreading these fields — writing to the wrong sheet
or an off-by-one range — causes silent failures that produce no error
message but score zero.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Datatype and datetime preservation (15/323 patches).

<div id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

Write dates and numeric values as native Python types, not strings. Both
<span id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">pandas</span> date parsing and
<span id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">openpyxl</span> cell assignment can
silently stringify datetime values; inspect each column’s dtype before
writing and use <span id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">openpyxl</span>’s native datetime
assignment.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Workbook structure exploration before editing (success-dominant, $`\sim`$<!-- -->13/323 patches).

<div id="A1.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para">

List all sheets, inspect row/column layout, and verify header positions
before any write. This pre-edit exploration prevents wrong-sheet and
wrong-range failures and accounts for a substantial share of the 151
success-leaning patches in the run.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2" class="section ltx_appendix" lang="en">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix B </span>Prompt Templates and Intermediate Outputs

<div id="A2.p1" class="ltx_para">

This appendix reproduces the key prompt templates used in each pipeline
stage and illustrates representative intermediate outputs to make the
pipeline fully transparent and reproducible.

</div>

<div id="A2.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">B.1 </span>Stage 1: Agent System Prompt Template

<div id="A2.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

The agent $`\pi_{\theta}`$ operates under the following system prompt
during trajectory collection. The skill $`\mathcal{S}_{0}`$ is prepended
to the user context at inference time. Note that this differs from
standard loading process of skills, where initially the agent only has
access to skill descriptions. We simplify this by preload the SKILL.md
content to system prompt because Trace2Skill focus on improving a fixed
target skill which is known relative to the task. Therefore, there is no
need for skill selection as the standard skill loading. Importantly, the
Trace2Skill skill using agent still need to procedurally discover
resources pointed by the preloaded SKILL.md (e.g., resources and
scripts), which are not preloaded.

</div>

<div id="A2.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">B.2 </span>Stage 2: Analyst Prompt Templates and Example Patches

<div id="A2.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

In Stage 2, the patch proposing agents first draw error and success
memory items similar to
(<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">ouyang2026reasoningbankscalingagentselfevolving</span>),
which are generalizable trajectory-level knowledge that might be helpful
for future task executions. Next, the agents read the original skill
directory and then propose a patch to encode the memory items into the
skill.

</div>

<div id="A2.SS2.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

<div id="A2.SS2.SSS3" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">B.2.3 </span>Example Trajectory-Level Patch (Error Analyst Output)

<div id="A2.SS2.SSS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Each analyst produces a structured patch for a single trajectory. The
following example is from an error analyst ($`\mathcal{A}^{-}`$)
processing one failure trajectory where the agent deleted rows outside
the specified range. The patch proposes adding a new
<span id="A2.SS2.SSS3.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">SKILL.md</span> section on
row-manipulation safety; this patch, along with 52 others citing related
structural-edit failures, is later consolidated into the
“Structural-edit safety” SoP (§<a href="#S4.SS4" class="ltx_ref"
title="4.4 Generalizable SoPs Learned ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ Trace2Skill: Distill Trajectory-Local Lessons into Transferable Agent Skills"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4.4</span></a>).

</div>

<div id="A2.SS2.SSS3.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<div id="A2.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">B.3 </span>Stage 3: Merge Operator Prompt and Example Consolidated Patch

<div id="A2.SS3.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">B.3.1 </span>Merge Operator Prompt ($`\mathcal{M}`$)

<div id="A2.SS3.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">B.3.2 </span>Example Final Consolidated Patch $`p^{*}`$ (After Full Merge Hierarchy)

<div id="A2.SS3.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

The following excerpt shows the reasoning and representative edits from
the final consolidated patch $`p^{*}`$ produced after four levels of
hierarchical merging over 323 individual trajectory patches on
SpreadsheetBench-Verified.

<div id="A2.SS3.SSS3" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">B.3.3 </span>Example Translated Diff: Final Consolidated Patch $`p^{*}`$

<div id="A2.SS3.SSS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

The JSON patch $`p^{*}`$ produced by Stage 3 is translated
programmatically into a unified diff and applied directly to the skill
directory. The following excerpt shows two hunks from the final applied
diff on SpreadsheetBench-Verified, corresponding to the two most
prevalent SoPs: formula recalculation enforcement (178/323 patches) and
tool-selection guidance (177/323 patches).

</div>

<div id="A2.SS3.SSS3.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">
